The bird is perched upon a white cube—a symbol of salt or the material world—which is decorated with **three red roses** <note>A classic Rosicrucian motif representing the unfolding of the soul and spiritual attainment within the physical realm.</note> arranged in a triangle. **HERMETIC PHILOSOPHY** <note>original: "PHILOSOPHIA HERMETICA"</note>

# Preface

fire <note>original: "Feuers." This completes the sentence from the previous page: "...if not for the force of the fire driving it out of this mineral?"</note> were to drive it out of this mineral? I know full well that we are not capable of conquering all bodies through fire and thereby unlocking their innermost essence. The noble metals <note>Gold and silver, which were known for their resistance to oxidation and acid.</note> are not changed by an ordinary kitchen fire; however, through **solar fire** <note>original: "Sonnenfeuer." In the 18th century, scientists used large burning lenses or mirrors to concentrate sunlight, achieving temperatures far higher than those of a standard furnace.</note>, they are transformed into a *glass*—a substance that cannot be further subdued by any fire, and which nevertheless reveals and allows us to recognize nothing further regarding their constituent parts.

In the meantime, we do indeed investigate the nature of many bodies through it [fire], at least with far greater convenience than can be achieved through water, which has been proposed and used by some instead of fire for physical and chemical experiments. One must, to be sure, in the chemical investigation of the <term>basic mixture</term> <note>original: "Grundmischung." This refers to the fundamental proportions of the primary elements or "principles" that make up a substance.</note>...

While often translated as "being," in this context it refers to the essential nature or "essence" of fire.</note> which expresses His highness so vividly that He even compares Himself to it in the Scripture <note>original: "Schrift." Since the opinions of naturalists <term>naturalists: *Naturforscher*—individuals who study the physical world and its laws, a term used before the modern word "scientist" became common.</term> regarding the nature and the essence of fire are so very different, one cannot easily say anything about it that would please everyone. This, however, must be done with modesty, and I believe that I have not overstepped its boundaries. The Republic of Letters <term>Republic of Letters: *Reich der Gelehrten*—the international community of scholars and intellectuals who exchanged ideas across borders during the 17th and 18th centuries.</term> is a republic in which no one is forbidden from presenting their thoughts on things which cannot be proven with undoubted certainty. It should surprise no one that another person views the world from a different perspective and, for that reason, has his own particular ideas <note>original: "Einfälle." While it can mean "sudden ideas" or "notions," in this context it refers to the author's specific theoretical insights.</note> on various matters. All mortals are linked together by the bond of sociability <note>original: "Geselligkeit." A key Enlightenment concept referring to the mutual support and civilized interaction between members of society.</note>, and I believe that this bond is not at all damaged by a contradiction, as long as no bitterness is mixed with it. I revere the great men who, through their laudable efforts, have [advanced] natural science <term>natural science: *Naturlehre*—the study of the laws of nature and the physical world, often referred to as "natural philosophy" during this period.</term> in our days to...

# Preface

In the doctrine of fire, I distinguish between elementary fire, the combustible <term>the combustible: *das Brenbare*—in 18th-century natural philosophy, this refers to the substance within bodies that enables them to burn, often associated with the theory of phlogiston.</term>, and light. I regard elementary fire as a substance <note>original: *Wesen*. While it can mean "being" or "essence," in this scientific context it refers to a physical entity or substance.</note> consisting of very subtle parts, all of which possess a repulsive force <term>repulsive force: *fortstoßende Kraft*—the power to push away or repel; a concept used by early scientists to explain how heat expands matter and how particles interact without touching.</term>, and which is spread throughout the entire structure of the world <term>structure of the world: *Weltbau*—the physical architecture of the universe or cosmos.</term>. From this, the combustible is composed. Light seems to be distinct from elementary fire and to consist of parts that, while they do not touch one another, also possess no repulsive force. And the electrical matter, which I discuss last, is, I believe, nothing other than a type of the combustible, which however

<summary>The author outlines his theoretical framework for physics, distinguishing between elementary fire, light, and "the combustible," while suggesting that electricity is a variation of flammable matter. Furthermore, I have resolved to investigate the theory of fire even more extensively and to strive to clarify this obscure matter ever more. This can happen in no other way than through experiments <term>experiments: *Versuche*—in the 18th century, this term encompassed trials, tests, and systematic scientific experiments.</term>: For however fortunately one may often guess in natural philosophy <term>natural philosophy: *Naturlehre*—the historical study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern physics.</term>; yet the world is so little improved by such new theoretical structures <term>theoretical structures: *Lehrgebäude*—literally "teaching-buildings," referring to the systematic construction of scientific or philosophical doctrines.</term> if these are not confirmed by experiments and corroborated by the testimony of the senses. This is because we discover what is actual in natural philosophy only through the senses, even though our intellect <term>intellect: *Verstand*—the cognitive faculty of reason used to process and judge sensory data.</term> teaches us to judge correctly regarding that which has been discovered through the senses.

# Preface.

However, such experiments <term>experiments: *Versuche*—referring to the practical trials or tests mentioned on the previous page.</term> require time and expense, and my current circumstances do not allow me to follow the impulse I feel—to learn the truth in this matter more closely—as I would wish. Should it please Providence <note>Providence: *Vorsehung*—a common 18th-century reference to divine guidance or the unfolding of one's fate.</note> to place me in different circumstances, I will perhaps confirm my current conjectures <term>conjectures: *Muthmaßungen*—preliminary hypotheses or educated guesses not yet fully proven by rigorous experimentation.</term> in a larger work and investigate this matter more precisely.

### §. 1.

The study of the elements of **bodies** <term>Körper: in this context, physical matter or solid objects</term> has always occupied the greatest **naturalists** <term>Naturforscher: scholars who studied the physical world and its laws before the modern term "scientist" was used</term>. People have not been satisfied merely with becoming acquainted with the phenomena of the **cosmos** <term>Weltbaues: literally "the building of the world," referring to the structure and architecture of the universe</term>; they have also endeavored to penetrate into the **knowledge** of their causes and to investigate the reasons why this or that **effect** <term>Wirkung: the action or result produced by a physical force</term> occurred in one way and not another. The effects of bodies often depend on the **shape** and power of the smallest **parts** <term>Theile: particles or components</term> from which they are composed: could one, therefore, possibly investigate the causes of their effects without looking at these small particles, which we call elements? It was therefore necessary to examine the elements from which bodies are composed. But has one, in this inves-

<summary>This introductory section argues that understanding the physical world requires investigating the fundamental "elements" or tiny particles that compose all matter. It suggests that the observable effects of objects are determined by the shape and power of these microscopic components.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, elements, corpuscular theory, causality, physics, structure of the universe, microscopic particles</keywords>

# Thoughts on Fire

->Thoughts on Fire<-

tigation been successful? One cannot answer this with a complete "Yes." The greatest **naturalists** <term>Naturlehrer: literally "nature teachers," referring to scholars who studied the physical world before the modern definition of "scientist" existed</term> are not yet in agreement when they are to determine the number, the nature, the shape, and the powers of these elements; and is it not a sign that a matter is still dark and hidden when such great men do not yet agree in their concepts of it? It is well known that the **scholastic teachers** <term>Schullehrer: refers to the academic authorities of the "schools" or universities who adhered to traditional medieval and Renaissance philosophy</term>, following the guidance of the great Aristotle, believed in four elements, whose authority has been maintained by many even up to our own times. At the very least, we still frequently do **fire, water, air, and earth** the honor of calling them the four elements. Aristotle had, as he believed, well-founded reasons why he established more than one element and why he contradicted those **philosophers** <term>Weltweisen: literally "the world-wise," a standard 18th-century German term for philosophers or natural scientists</term> who wanted to derive everything from a single primary element. The parts of an element must be of one kind; therefore, they must always produce uniform **effects** <term>Würkungen: the actions or results produced by a physical substance</term>. Now, if there were only one element, its effects would always have to be uniform. And how would one then explain the diversity of events in our **cosmos** <term>Weltbau: literally "the building of the world," referring to the structure and architecture of the universe</term>? There must be, so to speak, a conflict between the elements if diverse and opposing events are to occur. Why did [he] believe

<meta>catchword: but (aber)</meta>

<summary>The author notes that scholars still disagree on the nature of the elements, suggesting the topic remains mysterious. He explains Aristotle's reasoning for the four-element theory (fire, water, air, earth), arguing that a single element could not account for the diversity and conflict observed in the natural world.</summary>

One can easily guess the cause. In his investigation of bodies, he relied on the sense of touch—a sense that cannot easily deceive us because we touch the bodies directly. Now, the main properties of bodies that we discover through touch are: **warmth, cold, dryness, and moisture**. Yet touch presents fire to us as warm, air as cold, earth as dry, and water as moist. <note>In traditional Aristotelian philosophy, each element was actually defined by a pair of these qualities (e.g., Fire is hot and dry). The author here simplifies the system to a one-to-one correspondence to make his point.</note> What else could this great Greek conclude from such an observation than this: there must be four types of **primary matter** <term>Urstoff: the primordial or fundamental substance from which all physical things are composed</term> that caused these changes in the bodies?

But was Aristotle more justified in exploring the elements of bodies through touch than other **philosophers** <term>Weltweisen: literally "the world-wise," a standard 18th-century German term for natural philosophers or scientists</term> who relied on sight and believed in only two elements: light and darkness? The followers of **Epicurus** granted to the imagination what other philosophers assigned to the senses. They imagined an infinite number of elements and filled the empty space with a vast quantity of **sun-dust** <note>Sonnenstäubchen: literally "little sun-dust," referring to the tiny motes seen dancing in a sunbeam, which Epicurean physics used as a metaphor for atoms</note>, none of which was like any other. ->§. 2.<-

The **Chemists** <term>Chimici: early chemical practitioners, often alchemists, who transitioned from ancient philosophy to experimental science</term>, who began to overthrow the teachings and authority of **Aristotle**, dismissed his four elements from their role in forming bodies. In their place, they introduced three others, which they believed could be obtained from all bodies through fire. They believed all bodies were composed of **salt, sulfur, and mercury**. <note>These are known as the "Tria Prima" or three primary principles. In this system, sulfur represented flammability, mercury represented volatility/fluidity, and salt represented solidity/permanence.</note> **Basilius Valentinus**, a monk who is said to have lived in the 15th century and who diligently applied himself to the investigation of nature through fire, gave birth to this opinion—or rather, borrowed it from the Egyptians in a modified form. One can regard **Theophrastus Paracelsus** <note>A famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note> as the foster father of this theory, so to speak, because it was spread through him across almost all of Europe. But even these elements did not reign alone for long. **Willis** <note>Thomas Willis (1621–1675), an English doctor influential in the early Royal Society.</note> and **Homberg** <note>Wilhelm Homberg (1652–1715), a prominent chemist at the French Royal Academy of Sciences.</note> added earth and water to them, and the supposed mercury was soon transformed into a subtle spirit,

From these three elements, he derived the phenomena of the structure of the universe in an ingenious, but never proven, way. **Newton** seems—if we correctly understand the meaning of his words in the ***Optics*** <note>original: "Optic"</note> \*)—to have believed in only one element, which consisted of very small, dense, and differently shaped parts, from which the world arose solely through the working power of the Creator. # Thoughts on Fire.

->§. 3.<-

One should not be at all surprised by the disagreement that prevails among natural philosophers <term>Naturlehrer (natural teachers) — an 18th-century term for physicists or natural scientists</term> when they are tasked with defining the nature of each element specifically, since, as we have heard, they are not yet in agreement regarding the definition of elements in general. Indeed, they differ greatly in their opinions on the nature of fire. Some imagine fire as something special, as a distinct matter <note>This refers to the "substantive" theory of heat, later known as the caloric theory, which viewed heat as a physical fluid</note> that possesses a nature entirely different from all other matter. Others believe that nothing more is necessary for fire than the fineness <note>original: "Subtilität"</note> of its parts and rapid movement. They argue that any matter could become fire if it were broken down into particles small enough to penetrate the tiniest gaps while receiving the appropriate degree of motion <note>This "mechanical" or kinetic view was held by thinkers like Boyle and Hooke, who saw heat as a mode of motion rather than a substance</note>. Both sides argue with weighty reasons, and it will contribute not a little to the understanding of the nature of fire if we first apply ourselves to investigating which of the two opinions is the most probable.

### §. 3.

One should not be surprised at all by the disagreement that prevails among natural philosophers <term>natural philosophers: (original: *Naturlehrern*) scholars who studied the laws of nature and the physical world, a field that preceded modern physics and chemistry</term> when they are tasked with defining the nature of each element in particular, since, as we have heard, they are not yet in agreement regarding the definition of elements in general. In fact, they are very much divided in their opinions regarding the nature of fire. Some imagine fire as something special, as a distinct matter <term>matter: (original: *Materie*) the physical substance or "stuff" that makes up the universe</term> that possesses an essence entirely different from all other matter. Others believe that nothing else is required for fire than the fineness of the parts and rapid motion <note>The author is contrasting the "substance theory" (fire as a physical element) with the "kinetic theory" (fire as the result of rapidly moving particles), a major debate in 18th-century science</note>. Every kind of matter could become fire if it were broken down into such small parts that they could penetrate the smallest interstices <term>interstices: (original: *Zwischenräumlein*) the tiny, microscopic gaps or pores between the solid particles of a substance</term> and, in doing so, attained the proper degree of motion. Both sides argue with significant reasons, and it will contribute not a little to our knowledge of the nature of fire if we first make it our business to investigate which of the two opinions is the more probable.

<header>...and the bodies related thereto.</header>

The bottle with the board and light was lowered under the water. The outer air had a connection with the inner air through the tube attached to the board. Thus, even if some air had been consumed by the fire, this loss could have been replaced by the air entering through the tube, and the light would have remained burning. However, this did not happen; instead, it soon went out. One sees from this, as *Nieuwentit* <note>Bernard Nieuwentyt (1654–1718), whose experiments were foundational in early pneumatic chemistry.</note> judges, that the air itself is not required for the nourishment of the fire; rather, there is something *in* the air which did not have sufficient access here, and whose absence therefore caused the light to be extinguished.

One went even further. Through the tube attached to the opening in the board, air was blown into the bottle. Here, something strange was noticed. If the air was driven in by a bellows, the flame was maintained. But if the air—already warmed in the lungs—was blown in through the mouth, the light went out; and this happened all the more quickly the longer the air had remained in the lungs. Should it not follow from this that the air leaves behind in the lungs that which serves as nourishment for the flame? Indeed, *Nieuwentit* makes this conclusion. <note>This is a remarkably early observation of what we now know as the consumption of oxygen and the production of carbon dioxide during both respiration and combustion.</note> But it seems to me that one could [explain] this phenomenon—

For is there not a quantity of moisture separated from the blood in the lungs, which then mixes with the air? ### § 6.

It often happens that people argue over words without first having determined their meaning. Might it not have happened here too, in the dispute over the essence of fire? We would therefore do best to first agree on the concept of "essence" before we establish anything regarding the nature of fire. We find that the essence of bodies consists in the manner of their composition. Are not <term>spirit of nitre</term> <note>Nitric acid; a strong, corrosive mineral acid essential in early chemistry and alchemy.</note> and <term>spirit of vitriol</term> <note>Sulfuric acid; historically obtained by distilling "vitriol" or sulfate minerals.</note> different from one another in their essence? ## §. 9.

To determine the nature of fire, we must distinguish between three kinds of **matter** <note>The author uses "matter" here in the plural sense (*Materien*), referring to distinct physical substances or principles.</note> that nature has bound together, each of which is capable of acting on its own and producing a sensation within us. These are the **combustible**, the **elementary fire**, and **light**, which come together at the creation of a **flame**. Since the effects of fire become particularly perceptible during the creation of a **flame**, it will, I hope, not be ill-advised if we first consider the origin of a **flame** in order to more closely understand and define the properties of fire.

## §. 10.

A **flame** usually arises when a certain type of light **bodies**, which tend to be **oily** or **sulfurous** <note>In 18th-century natural philosophy, "sulfurous" often described any oily, flammable substance thought to contain the principle of fire, rather than just the chemical element sulfur.</note>, are set into such a motion that separates their **elementary parts**,

A solid body can therefore become liquid if the cohesion of its parts is reduced, and this can happen if its elements move further away from one another. Because the elements of matter attract one another, and this attraction becomes weaker the further they are from each other, a body must become liquid when its parts distance themselves from one another. We find the same thing in the melting of metals, and also in other bodies that are lighter than these. Experience teaches,

# Thoughts on Fire

are exactly what we call the **combustible**. <note>Original: *Brenbare*. In 18th-century natural philosophy, this term refers to the specific substance or principle within a body that allows it to catch fire.</note> As soon as this combustible matter is properly freed from the other constituent parts, it produces three kinds of phenomena, from which its nature and character can be more closely recognized and determined.

We find, firstly, that the freed combustible matter constitutes a fluid body, which is pressed into a specific shape by the air.

We find, secondly, that this fluid body acts upon our <term>sensory nerves</term>, <note>Original: *Gefühlsnerven*. The author is describing the physiological response to thermal energy.</note> producing in them the sensation of warmth, a heat, and finally even a pain. This sensation, however, can occur in two ways. It happens 1) when we approach the flame so closely that it directly touches one of our limbs. But it also happens 2) when we are not situated quite so close to the flame, although the sensation is strongest upon contact with the flame.

Finally, we observe thirdly that the parts of the flame produce a sensation in our <term>optic nerves</term> <note>Original: *Gesichtsnerven*. Literally "nerves of sight."</note> even at an uncommonly great distance, and thus become visible. We intend to go through and explain these phenomena individually.

## §. 12.

1) The released **combustible** <note>Original: *Brenbare*. This refers to the specific substance or "principle" within a material that allows it to catch fire, a central concept in 18th-century chemistry.</note> constitutes a distinct fluid body. Now, the parts of a fluid body adhere to one another poorly; therefore, the cohesion of the combustible matter that makes up the flame must not be very great. I must first say a word to my readers about fluid bodies in general, so that I might better explain and make understandable what is to be said hereafter regarding fire.

We say that the parts of a fluid body do not hold together strongly. It is therefore common to imagine that the smallest particles of the elements of fluid bodies are spherical. Since two spheres touch at only a single point—and cohesion, as is generally assumed, depends on contact—then the cohesion must certainly be weaker the fewer points of contact there are. And must there not then be the least amount of cohesion in a circular shape?

However, I do not know how one would explain the **liquefaction** <note>Original: *Flüssigmachung*. The process of turning a solid into a liquid, such as melting.</note> of many otherwise solid bodies using this theory, as even metals themselves can be made fluid, even though it is not at all likely that their ele-

### and those related bodies.

...surmountable difficulties. However, one commonly fixes an opinion in one's head and afterwards forces all the phenomena of nature to fit it, even though one must often enough torture oneself to do so. Water indeed seems to consist of <term>particles</term> <note>Original: *Theilchen*. Small units of matter; in this era, scientists were debating whether these were actual atoms or just tiny droplets.</note> that touch one another and constitute a liquid body only because they are indescribably small and have a round shape; they therefore touch at only a single point and thus have poor cohesion.
Water cannot be compressed at all, or forced into a narrower space by any external force. This very fact makes it highly probable that its elements, or smallest parts, touch each other directly. In that case, it is certainly no wonder that they cannot be compressed, because <term>matter</term> <note>Original: *Materie*.</note> is <term>impenetrable</term> <note>Original: *undurchdringlich*. move away from each other; this we call **repulsion**, the other **attraction**. Imagine several parts of **matter** that strive to move away from one another, or repel. Since we perceive that the **forces** of matter are finite, and consequently have their specific limits and their **measure**, we can assume that at a certain distance, the **effect** of bodies on each other becomes infinitely small and is to be regarded as nothing. Therefore, if two **elements** of matter move away from each other, they will continue to do so as long as their effect on each other is noticeable. But suppose they were hindered in their **movement**, which can happen in many ways; they would still have a constant effort to move away from each other, and this effort will become active as soon as this obstacle is removed. Since the forces of matter are not infinite but have their specific measure, one can also assume that a force acts upon these two repelling parts of matter in an opposite **direction**, and that this force is greater than the repelling force of the particles of matter. A greater force, when it [acts] in an opposite— <note>The sentence continues on the next page with the word "opposed."</note>

direction, acting simultaneously with a smaller one, it not only cancels out the effect of the smaller [force], but also continues to act upon the body with the force that remains after the smaller has been subtracted from the larger. Therefore, if two parts of **matter** repel one another, but another greater **force** acts in the opposite direction upon these parts of matter, they will not only fail to move away from each other, but will actually approach each other until the **repulsive force** <note>original: "zurückstossende Kraft"</note> has reached **equilibrium** with the force acting in the opposite direction. For since the **effect** of matter upon itself decreases with distance, it must also become stronger through proximity. Thus, if a force acting upon two **particles** <note>original: "Körperchen," literally "small bodies"</note> that are striving to move away from each other is stronger than the force with which they repel each other at a certain distance: it can nevertheless happen that when both bodies have come closer to each other, and their distance thus decreases, the repulsive force becomes stronger and thereby maintains equilibrium with the other force.

**particles** <insert>are</insert> larger than the **water particles**; then they would have to be significantly lighter than the **water particles** if they touched each other directly. For **air** is nearly a thousand times lighter than **water**: if one assumes that the smallest particles of both fluid bodies were of the same **density**, then the air could not be lighter than the water. In this context, it refers to the specific gravity or the weight of an individual particle.</term> at the same density, would relate as 1 to 8. <note>The author is applying the square-cube law: if the diameter of a sphere doubles, its volume (and thus its mass/weight at equal density) increases by the cube of the ratio (2³ = 8).</note> Therefore, in a space where one air particle is, eight water particles would have room, and consequently the weight of both bodies would be equal. One will easily understand from this that since the weight of both bodies is so notably different, the smallest particles of air must be significantly lighter than the water particles; they must at the very least also be a thousand times lighter than the water particles. But if two bodies of the same size have different weights, then the masses, or the densities, relate to each other as the weights, so that the body that is a thousand times heavier must also be a thousand times denser. If, however, the sizes are not the same, then they relate as the **quotients** of the weights by the size—

->§. 18.<-

I must now attempt to explain the properties of fire more clearly to my readers. Since we regard fire as a fluid substance that is uncommonly subtle and spread throughout the entire universe, one can easily understand that such a fluid substance cannot come to rest until it has spread itself uniformly through all bodies. Now, every particle of matter has its specific <term>sphere of action<note>original: "Würkungsphäre" - This is the microscopic field or radius within which a particle's forces (like gravity or repulsion) can be felt by other particles.</note></term>: For the action of bodies, as is well known, decreases with distance and therefore becomes imperceptible at a great distance. Exactly the same must also apply to these <term>fire particles<note>original: "Feuertheilchen"</note></term>. If, therefore, a fire particle approaches a solid part of a body, it will endeavor to push it away; however, because the parts of a solid body are connected to one another and thus resist the weak action of a fire particle, the fire particle must bounce back from the body. Now, however, one will say: fire is never at rest; for do not bodies on our Earth burn constantly? I fully understand all of this: but are we not permitted to join certain concepts together, or to separate them from one another, in order to derive other truths from them in an orderly fashion, even if nature itself either never joins or never separates these things? Mathematics gives us an obvious and most advantageous example, wherein the length, the height, and the thickness of bodies are separated, and each is considered individually, even though we never perceive this separation in nature. This very same thing is permitted to us in <term>natural science<note>original: "Naturlehre" - the historical term for the study of nature and physics.</note></term>, and it happens daily in <term>astronomy<note>original: "Astronomie"</note></term>. ->§ 20.<-

If the fire is at complete rest, and its parts are therefore equidistant from one another; let us suppose that one of these parts were set in motion, and let us see what consequences this will entail. The moved <term>fire-particle<note>original: "Feuertheilchen" - the author describes fire as a "fluid" composed of individual physical particles, a central concept in 18th-century corpuscular physics.</note></term> must move away from some to which it was first near, and on the other hand, approach others all the more. Since it moves away from the parts located behind it, the <term>resistance<note>original: "Widerstand" - the force or presence of other particles that maintains equilibrium.</note></term> is lifted which kept the fire-parts located behind it from moving; consequently, these will set themselves in motion once the resistance is lifted. However, since it also approaches those located in front of it, it must push them forward. Therefore, the fire-parts that lie in a straight line with the <term>direction<note>original: "Direction" - used here to mean the vector or specific path of motion.</note></term> of the one set in motion will be moved toward all sides. However, since the fire-particle, once set in motion, not only pushes away the fire-parts located next to it, but is also pushed back by these same parts in an opposite direction: it is no wonder if the <term>moving force<note>original: "bewegende Kraft" - the kinetic energy or momentum driving the particle.</note></term> which drives it becomes smaller, and the motion thus decreases. ### §. 23.

When the <term>combustible parts<note>original: "brenbaren Theile" — referring to the substances within a material (often associated with the historical theory of phlogiston) that are released during the process of burning.</note></term> of a body are released in large quantities from their connection with the rest of the matter; they must seek to move away from one another, and they will indeed do so, if they are not hindered in their movement by another body. If, however, they are held together by a body outside of them, they must constitute such a body that possesses all the properties of a <term>fluid, elastic being<note>original: "flüssigen elastischen Wesens" — in 18th-century physics, "fluid" meant any substance that flows (including gases), and "elastic" meant it could be compressed and then expand back to its original volume.</note></term> described in §. 14. For it will allow itself to be compressed, but it will also expand again when the external pressure ceases, because all its parts will then truly move away from one another. Now my readers will see without great difficulty that I intend to describe a flame to them, and I must indicate this similarity even more clearly. That a flame is a <term>fluid body<note>original: "flüssiger Körper"</note></term> I need to prove all the less, since this matter is quite established among almost all <term>natural philosophers<note>original: "Naturkündiger" — the 18th-century term for scientists who studied the physical laws of the natural world.</note></term>. dy is not continued in straight lines, but rather it spreads through the whole body in all directions once a single particle has been set in motion. One sees this clearly in water and in air. One hears the sound of someone speaking outside in every part of a room, even if the door is closed and the movement of the air must propagate through the narrow cracks of the door. In this case, a rectilinear <note>Straight-line.</note> motion of sound is not to be thought of at all. Now, if light were to follow this very same law, then no darkness at all would be possible on the face of the earth. It would be day at all times and never become night. For since light is constantly in motion, it would move in all directions within the cosmic structure <note>original: "Weltgebäude" — literally "the building of the world," a common 18th-century term for the universe or cosmos.</note>, and would therefore illuminate our atmosphere by night as well as by day. This, however, does not happen, and experience teaches that light moves in no other way than in straight lines. One concludes, therefore, that it is not a fluid body \*). I believe, however, that one [can regard] light as a fluid, spread through the whole—

# Thoughts on Fire

The diamond, the so well-known <term>Bolognese stone<note>original: "Bononienserstein" — a type of barite found near Bologna, Italy, famous in the 17th and 18th centuries for its phosphorescent properties.</note></term>, and other similar bodies that shine in the dark when they have previously been exposed to the rays of the sun, testify to exactly this. For how should these be able to act upon the light if it did not surround them on all sides? And how would this be possible if it were not spread through the entire <term>cosmic space<note>original: "Weltraum" — referring to the vast expanse of the universe beyond the immediate atmosphere.</note></term>? I know well that many imagine the matter in such a way that similar bodies, like a sponge, draw the light of the sun into themselves and afterwards give it off again in the dark. However, I do not see at all what probable causes can be given for this soaking up <note>original: "Einsaugens" — literally "sucking in."</note> and blowing out <note>original: "Ausblasens,"</note> since it is much more probable that the smallest parts of such bodies are agitated <note>original: "erschüttert" — refers to a mechanical vibration or shaking of particles.</note> by the light of the sun, and lose this same agitation very slowly because of their velocity. Through this continuous agitation of their parts, they become capable of acting upon the light surrounding them, pushing the same away from themselves, and thereby [producing] a sensation in our eyes

Nature always tends to take the shortest path, and I believe that naturalists <note>original: "Naturforscher" — a term used in the 18th century for scientists who studied the physical world and its phenomena.</note> must observe this very same maxim. ### and related bodies.

Minds such as *Newton’s* were content to point out the simple phenomena of nature and to explain everything that could be asserted with certainty about them. Perhaps they later watched with pleasure as others—who stand on their shoulders <note>A reference to Newton's famous 1675 statement: "If I have seen further it is by standing on the shoulders of Giants."</note> and accept their discoveries with admiration and astonishment—set to work on these matters, wanting to explain that which the great minds themselves did not dare to venture, for fear of error. In this regard, *Newton* has also granted us the joy of occupying ourselves with the investigation of the causes of these phenomena and racking our brains over them. It is certainly infinitely difficult for someone who wishes to ponder the causes of natural phenomena to imagine such a force about which nothing more is said than that it exists. And what a wonderful property of matter it would have to be, that it could attract light rays but also repel them, without the rays actually striking the solid parts of the bodies and being reflected by them. One must necessarily assume that light is attracted by these bodies. The refraction <term>refraction: the bending of light as it passes from one medium to another</term> of rays, the resulting separation of them from one another, and other phenomena, teach—

# ...and those bodies related to them.

Nothing is more certain than that light also spreads throughout the entire structure of the world through a constant outflow from the sun. Since light moves with extraordinary speed, it must collide with the **elementary fire particles** <term>elementary fire particles: according to the 18th-century "caloric" or fluid theory of heat, these were the microscopic constituents of a subtle fluid that filled all space and mediated heat and light</term> scattered throughout the space of the heavens. And because the force with which light moves is great, the direction of the light's motion will be little changed by the repulsive force of the fire particles; on the contrary, the fire particles will move quickly away from the light particles that are in motion.

This displacement will occur without difficulty as long as no body is present that resists the motion of the fire particles, stops them, and prevents them from avoiding the light. In the space of the heavens, which appears to be empty of other matter besides fire and light, light will therefore move without hindrance because the fire particles have room to get out of its way.

But suppose a ray of light moves toward a solid body; then the fire particle situated closest to the body will not be able to easily avoid the motion of the light ray: it will thus be squeezed into a narrow space between the solid parts of the body and the light particle. The solid parts of the body are impenetrable; the fire-

From all this, he concludes that bodies act upon light through a repulsion <term>repulsion: a pushing-back force, referred to as "Zurükstoßen" in the original German text</term>. For since the shadow is wider than it ought to be, the rays must be pushed further apart as they pass by the body; and since colors arise when light rays of different kinds—which move with different force—are separated from one another, one can easily see that this separation must occur if a body acts upon them with an equal force. For then those that move with the greatest force, or as is most probable, with the greatest speed, are refracted <term>refracted: bent from a straight path; the author uses this to describe the deviation caused by repulsion</term> less out of their course than the others moving with less force or more slowly. ...approaches, and brings it <note>Referring to the light ray mentioned at the end of the previous page.</note> out of its path: therefore, this ray moves in a different direction through the transparent body than it had before it was so close to the body that the latter could act upon it. Now, the attractive force of a body is proportional to the quantity of its parts or its mass; at least this law holds in most bodies that possess an attractive force. Thus, if a transparent body possesses much mass—that is, many solid parts—it must also strongly refract the rays. But if a body has many solid parts, more **fire-particles** <term>fire-particles: original "Feuertheile," referring to the 18th-century belief that fire consisted of physical corpuscles or a fluid (caloric) that interacted with light</term> moved by the light must strike against them, and these must therefore act upon the light in the manner previously explained. For this very reason, however, many light rays must also be thrown back; that is, the **reflection** of light must be strong.

I must address another objection. One might perhaps confront me with the well-known phenomenon of a paper soaked in oil. It is well-known that when paper, which is inherently opaque, is soaked with oil, it becomes transparent. Now, it is certain that the oil fills the small gaps <note>original: "Zwischenräumchen," literally "little intermediate spaces" or interstices</note> of the paper,

...and consequently increases its mass. But must not the number of its solid parts be increased with the increased mass of a body? Must not more light rays then also be reflected? For the transparency of a body, a free passage of light through its small gaps <note>original: "Zwischenräumchen," referring to the pores or interstices within the material</note> is required. Therefore, the fewer light rays that pass freely through a body, the less transparent it must be. If more rays are reflected by a body, then fewer must go through its small gaps: consequently, it seems that since more rays are reflected from a paper soaked in oil than from another, this paper must also be less transparent.

This objection is resolved if one considers the cause of transparency that <term>Newton</term> <note>Sir Isaac Newton (1642–1726), whose *Opticks* (1704) proposed that transparency depends on the uniform density and size of a material's internal particles.</note> has shown us. A body is transparent if the thickness of the parts of which it consists is such that light is not refracted very differently, but uniformly. For if it is refracted very differently, it must move in various directions within the body, and can consequently more easily strike against its solid parts and adhere to them. # Thoughts on Fire

act, and who does not see that the sum of all forces of the fire-particles, taken together, must be greater than the force of each one in particular? We can therefore assume that the combustible matter <note>original: "das Brenbare"; in the 18th century, this usually referred to phlogiston, the substance believed to be the material of fire</note> is composed of various fire-particles. I see well, however, that my readers will say: how can the elementary fire-particles <note>original: "Elementarfeuertheile"</note> combine with one another and be brought into a cohesive state, since they constantly seek to distance themselves from one another?

We must therefore certainly concern ourselves with a substance that links the fire-particles together. We find that all combustible bodies are composed of water and earth, or also often of salts. Oil, tallow <note>original: "Unschlit"; rendered animal fat used for making candles and soap</note>, sulfur, camphor—even wood, linen, paper, and whatever is capable of producing a flame—all possess other parts of matter besides the combustible substance within them.

If one now imagines that between these fire-particles, or around them, particles of other matter place themselves—which strongly cohere with one another and therefore hinder the fire-particles from separating—then one has everything necessary for the composition of combustible matter. That it is possible, however, for parts that repel each other and [seek] to remove themselves from one another...

# Thoughts on Fire

Their repulsive force, however, is smaller than the cohesion of the water particles. Therefore, even if the external air is pumped away, these simple air particles still cannot escape from the water, because they are not capable of breaking the cohesion of the water particles. However, that such simple air particles are indeed present between the water spheres <note>original: "Wasserkugeln"; natural philosophers of this era often envisioned fluid particles as tiny, perfect spheres</note> is evidenced by the elasticity of dissolved water. For as soon as the water is dissolved into a vapor <note>original: "Dunst"; here referring to steam or water vapor</note> by the power of fire, this vapor becomes elastic and possesses an uncommonly strong expansive force. Opportunities have even been taken to build large machines at mines and for other uses, which were driven solely by the powerfully expanding vapor of water; however, as far as I know, these have mostly fallen out of use again due to the expenses required to maintain the necessary fire <note>The author is likely referring to early atmospheric steam engines, such as the Newcomen engine, which were used to pump water from mines but required vast amounts of coal to operate.</note>. Furthermore, it is known to those not entirely inexperienced in chemistry <note>original: "Chimie"</note> that the expansive force of water dissolved by the power of fire is exceptionally strong. ->...and those bodies related to them.<-

Let us imagine the third figure, in which A, B, C, and D represent earthy or other particles of matter which touch at many points and must therefore adhere strongly together. If some <term>fire particles</term> <note>original: *Feuertheile*; 18th-century scientists often viewed fire as a physical substance made of tiny "corpuscles" or particles</note> are located between these, but are unable to sever the connection between parts A, B, C, and D, then these together will form the kind of body to which we give the name <term>combustible</term>. It is easy to see from this that the nature of combustible matter can be very diverse. For according to whether the parts A, B, C, and D are larger or smaller; according to whether they adhere more or less strongly; and finally, according to whether more or fewer elementary fire particles are trapped between them, the resulting effect must vary. And since we judge the nature of things by their effects, their nature must also appear diverse to us. I hope that none of my readers will see it as a contradiction that I compose the parts of combustible matter from so many other parts, yet simultaneously claim that they are very subtle <note>In historical scientific texts, "subtle" refers to matter that is extremely fine, thin, or capable of penetrating other substances</note>. ->§. 34.<-

Let one now imagine that some <term>elementary fire particles</term> <note>original: *Elementarfeuertheile*; fire was often viewed as a physical substance made of tiny "corpuscles"</note> happen to fall between the parts of other matter that strongly cohere with one another; thus they will be trapped in the same way that happens to air particles in water. If the cohesion of these foreign parts is stronger than the repulsive force of the fire particles, it follows that their cohesion cannot be overcome by the power of the fire particles. Consequently, these fire particles will not be able to exert their force upon other bodies until the connection of the parts in which they are trapped has been severed. <term>Combustible matter</term> <note>original: *Das Brenbare*</note>, as one sees from this observation, is distinguished from fire only in that it is larger than elementary fire—being composed of several parts of it—and that, besides the parts of elementary fire, combustible matter also contains foreign parts that are strongly coherent.

flame, all colors must necessarily be present: because the white color arises from the mixing of all the others: so it follows, that the <term>impulse</term> <note>original: *Stos* (Stoß); a physical push or impact transferred from one particle to another</note> with which the <term>combustible</term> <note>original: *Brenbare*; the flammable principle or substance within an object</note> acts upon the light is not entirely uniform. The velocity of the moving light depends on the impulse of the combustible: what follows more naturally from this, than that this impulse itself must also be different? This difference in the effect of the combustible upon the light can be viewed in two ways. For the combustible is so constituted that the <term>force</term> <note>original: *Kraft*; the power or energy exerted by the matter</note> with which it acts upon the light can either increase or not. I will explain myself more clearly. A combustible particle **A** can act upon the light with force = **v**; another particle **B** can move the light with force = **2 v**. Since **B** can move the light with force = **2 v**, there is no doubt that it should also be able to act upon the light with force **v**. We see this clearly in a burning candle. The lower part of the flame is blue. I have already shown in my *Essay on a Closer Explanation of the Nature of Colors*, page 50, that

# Thoughts on Fire

one must derive this phenomenon solely from the fact that the <term>combustible parts</term> <note>original: *brenbaren Theile*; the individual particles of flammable matter</note> do not receive the strongest degree of <term>vibration</term> <note>original: *Zitterung*; a term used in historical physics to describe the rapid oscillatory motion of particles that produces heat or light</note>—that is, their effect upon the light—all at once, but rather gradually. The closer they come to the flame, the stronger their vibration becomes: and this must also happen the higher they rise within it. Since they are only just entering the flame, their power to push the light forth must be smaller than when they rise higher within the same. Blue light moves the slowest; thus, the lower part of the flame must indeed appear blue. The cause of this different effect of the combustible is easy to understand. For since this consists of <term>fire particles</term> <note>original: *Feuertheilchen*; in the 18th century, heat and fire were often conceptualized as a physical substance or "fluid" made of tiny particles</note> that are compressed between parts of <term>foreign matter</term> <note>original: *fremder Materie*; non-combustible substances or different elements mixed with the fire-matter</note>; so also, depending on whether it consists of more or fewer fire particles, its power must be different. Suppose the space in which the fire particles are locked up is so large that only, for example, 3 fire particles had room in it; then the power of this combustible would be equal to the quantity of fire particles, that is, it would also = 3. If the space is larger, so that 5 fire parts have room in it; then its power must also be greater and = 5. In general, it

...and related bodies. 91

move the light, which gradually becomes greater (§ 35); thus we must strive to determine the **cause** of this phenomenon. It can be explained in the following manner. The parts of **matter** that enclose the **fire-particles** within themselves are so strongly connected to one another that the force of the fire contained in them is unable to separate them. The fire-particles are prevented from exerting their **repulsive force** <note>original: *fortstossende Kraft*; the author uses a Newtonian framework where particles either attract or repel one another</note> by this surrounding **rind** <note>original: *Rinde*; literally "bark" or "crust," used here to describe a physical casing or shell of matter that traps the fire within</note>. However, if these parts are separated by an external force, then the fire must be liberated and spread all the more strongly the closer those fire-particles that repel one another are brought together. For the repulsive force of the **elementary fire-particles** decreases with their distance and becomes greater as they approach one another (§ 35); it must therefore be greatest when they actually touch each other. Now, if the tearing of this **rind**—or to speak more precisely, the displacement of the foreign particles that enclose the fire—happens gradually, then its force must also manifest itself gradually. But this very fact teaches us that the **combustible matter** <term>Brenbare: a term used in the 18th century to describe the substance of fire, often associated with the Phlogiston theory, which suggested that flammability was a physical ingredient in objects</term> brought into action in this way must not act nearly so violently as when the

Therefore, when the rind is completely separated, the fire-particles no longer repel each other nearly as strongly as they would have done had they still been located very close to one another. But if the rind is **suddenly** broken, then the fire-particles still possess their full repulsive force and must move away with far greater violence, and thus set the light present everywhere into much greater motion.

It cannot be denied that a vast amount of combustible matter is found in the **womb of the earth**. The combustible minerals and the volatile **ores** <note>original: *Bergarten*; literally "mountain-types" or rock varieties containing minerals</note>, which owe their volatility for the most part to this essence, provide irrefutable evidence of this. # 98 Thoughts on Fire

...which usually occur partly in the flame, and partly in warm or heated bodies. We wish to consider the phenomena of the flame first. We notice here, in the first place, that the heat in the flame is not entirely equal throughout. In the lower part of the flame, and on its sides, it is weakest, but in the tip, or the upper part, it is strongest. This cannot be fully explained by the fact that the **combustible particles** <note>original: *brenbare Theile*</note> are only gradually heated the higher they rise in the flame, although this contributes not a little to it. For even if one wished to derive the cause from this—explaining why the flame is not as hot at the bottom as at the top—one would still not recognize from this why the heat is lower at the sides. We must, therefore, derive this occurrence primarily from another cause. When the **combustible particle** touches the flame, this initially happens only on the side facing the flame. For the **combustible matter** is, like all parts of **matter**, impenetrable <note>original: *undurchdringlich*; a fundamental principle in 18th-century physics stating that two objects cannot occupy the same space simultaneously, meaning the fire must act on the surface of a particle first</note>, and therefore it cannot touch the flame throughout its entire **substance**, but only on one side. As soon as the flame touches the combustible matter, it begins

and bodies related to them.

to act upon it, and separates its crust <note>original: *Rinde*; the author envisions combustible particles as having an outer shell that holds the "fire" inside</note>. Since this occurs first on the side that is turned toward the flame, the parts of the crust on this side are also separated first. Now, the **elementary fire** <note>original: *Elementarfeuer*; a 18th-century concept referring to fire as a primary material substance or "fluid" trapped within combustible objects</note> enclosed within this crust must move first toward the region where the resistance is weakest. Therefore, the **elementary fire** will break out from its container in the upper part first, and consequently, the **fire particles** will mostly move upwards. **Rockets**, **cannons**, and similar cylinders filled with **gunpowder** teach us this every day. For in these, the fire moves most forcefully toward where there is the least resistance; that is, it shoots out of their muzzle. Since the entire flame consists of nothing but such ruptured **combustible particles**, it is no wonder that the **elementary fire particles** within it mostly move upwards. The sensation of **heat** depends upon the movement of these **elementary fire particles**; must this sensation therefore not be strongest in the upper part of the flame?

# Thoughts on Fire

->§. 39.<-

For this very reason, we will also be able to derive and determine the shape <note>original: *Figur*</note> of the flame. Experience teaches us that the shape of the flame is always similar to a **pyramid**, whose base is near the burning body, while its tip is located much further above it. The flame is a **fluid body** <term>fluid body (flüssiger Körper): in early physics, this term referred to any substance that flows, including both liquids and gases</term> that is lighter than air (§. 23.); it must therefore float in it. Now we know that a fluid body floating in another—if the pressure of the other is equally great on all sides against it—takes on a **spherical shape**; and it would therefore seem that this should also reasonably occur with a flame. However, it is known from **natural philosophy** <note>original: *Naturlehre*; a historical term for the study of physics and the natural world</note> that pressure and counter-pressure are always equal to one another, and that a body which resists another with unequal force must also be pressed with unequal force. If, however, the pressure of a fluid body upon another fluid body floating within it is unequal, then the pressed body cannot maintain a spherical shape, because this requires a pressure that is equally large on all sides. Now, we have seen in the preceding that the

And what else follows from this, than that the flame must be far more compressed in the middle, and thus must be narrow *)?

# Thoughts on Fire

must here not only look at the coldness of the wax or <term>tallow (Unschlitt): rendered animal fat used to make cheaper candles before the advent of paraffin</term>, which, as Mr. *Musschenbroek* \*) rightly says, first requires a specific degree of heat before it can burn, and thus does not begin to burn immediately because there are various degrees of heat between the body of the candle and the flame: but I believe that one could also, not without cause, appeal here to the repulsive force of the flame. For the flame consists of nothing but parts that strive to move away from one another. The outermost parts of the flame will therefore also endeavor to push away the bodies lying closest to them. However, if these are immovable, the flame itself must move away from them. Since the burning candle is immovable and cannot give way to the weak push of the flame, the flame itself will move away from the candle. But since the air resists the movement of the flame from above, this removal will only occur until the resistance of the air holds a balance with the pushing force of the flame. For it is certain that the air [resists] the movement of the

candle <note>The text continues the sentence from the previous page: "took a tallow candle..."</note> of about 4 **Loth** <term>Loth: a pre-metric unit of weight used in German-speaking lands; 4 Loth is roughly equivalent to 60 grams or 2 ounces</term>, placed into a **receiver** <note>original: "Recipienten," the glass jar or chamber of a vacuum pump</note>, and began to pump the air out. The candle was extinguished within 2 minutes, but the flame, as the air was being pumped out, rose higher and higher on the **wick** until it reached its tip and was extinguished there. As soon as some air was drawn out of the receiver, its force necessarily had to decrease: it could therefore no longer resist the movement of the flame as strongly, and the flame thus gained the freedom to move further away from the candle <note>original: "Licht" (light), commonly used in this period to refer to a candle</note>, that is, to climb higher up the wick. But since this distance finally became so great that the rising **tallow particles** <note>original: "Unschlittheile"</note> could not receive the proper degree of heat required for burning, the flame naturally had to go out for lack of nourishment <note>i.e., fuel</note>. **Boyle** notes, however, as something special, that the flame not only became smaller the further it moved from the tallow candle, but that it finally appeared completely blue. Both of these phenomena can be explained without difficulty. For the further the flame moves from the tallow, the less the candle can be heated by it. Therefore, fewer tallow particles become liquid

# Thoughts on Fire.

...thereby the **elementary fire** <term>Elementarfeuer: in 18th-century physics, fire was often considered a fundamental element or fluid that existed within substances</term> contained within them is released and spreads out, and sets in motion not only the elementary fire found everywhere, but also the **light**. Who does not see that this movement would not last long unless new **combustible parts** <note>original: "brenbare Theile"</note> were constantly supplied to the flame? These combustible parts supplied to the flame are usually called the **nourishment of fire**: because the flame cannot exist without them.

However, besides this nourishment, experience also teaches that the **air** is indispensable for the maintenance of the flame. For in the experiment of *Boyle* <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691), who conducted famous experiments with vacuum pumps</note> cited earlier, the light <note>The author uses "Licht" (light) here to mean the candle flame itself</note> went out as soon as air was lacking under the **receiver** <term>Recipient: the glass bell jar used in vacuum experiments to create an airtight seal</term>. It is therefore believed that there is also something in the air that serves as a special nourishment for the flame. 

Now, I will not entirely deny that the combustible parts constantly flying around in the air—released from **vegetable matter** <note>original: "Vegetabilien"</note> or from decaying **animal matter** <note>original: "Animalien"</note>—might by chance nourish the flame. However, I must confess that I [view] the special nourishment of fire found in the air, as *Musschenbroek* <note>Petrus van Musschenbroek (1692–1761), a Dutch scientist famous for his work on electricity and physics</note> *) and with him very many of the latest **naturalists**—

For one must not assume anything in **natural science** <term>Naturlehre: a 18th-century term for physics and the study of the natural world</term> out of mere convenience <note>original: "vor die lange Weile," literally "for boredom," implying the invention of theories just to fill gaps in knowledge</note>, and if one must indeed assume something that cannot be sufficiently explained, then no other mode of explanation must be possible, or all other modes of explanation must possess a far lesser degree of **probability**. ### §. 43.

I must here address a seeming objection that could be raised against the **repulsion** <note>original: "Zurükstossen."</note> of the flame. One might say: if the flame possesses a repulsive force, then two flames that are very close to each other must move away from one another. For each flame would act upon the other through repulsion. Experience, however, testifies to the contrary: for two flames that are very close to each other seem to attract one another and actually unite. But it is nothing unusual in **natural science** <term>natural science (Naturlehre): the historical study of nature and the physical world, encompassing what we now categorize as physics and chemistry.</term> for events to occur that seem to be based on an attraction, and yet are actually derived solely from the **pressure of the air**. ### §. 44.

Hitherto we have considered the flame and seen what is required for its emergence. We find, however, that not all bodies are suited to be dissolved into a flame. In the meantime, they can nevertheless be heated, and when they have reached the highest degree of heat, they begin to produce light; we then say that they are **glowing**. Let us consider this glowing of bodies and investigate what this change in them consists of.

### §. 45.

When we consider glowing bodies, we will immediately notice two types of them. Some bodies lose something of their mass while glowing, while others are not changed by it at all and remain, once they become cold again, as heavy and as dense as they were before. **Iron** is changed little by glowing. It does indeed expand as long as it glows, but it

### §. 46.

Since glowing is nothing other than a very high degree of heat within a body, we must first investigate the nature of heat itself in order to account for this glowing. Since our concept of heat arises when the **elementary fire particles** <note>original: "Elementarfeuertheile." This refers to the contemporary belief in fire as a material substance or "fluid" made of tiny particles that could permeate other matter.</note> act upon our nerves with a certain force, we can conclude that in every body that appears warm to us, a movement of these elementary fire particles is occurring. This, however, can happen in two ways. For if the body possesses no combustible parts, it can only act upon the elementary fire through a vibration <note>original: "Erschütterung"; a shaking or mechanical agitation of the particles.</note> of its own parts. But if the body itself possesses combustible parts, then these can be set free, and

### §. 48.

Two bodies, which possess different **densities**, attain different degrees of **heat** if they are exposed to fire, or to the rays of the sun, or if both are rubbed for the same length of time. That body which possesses more **mass** within the same **volume** becomes warmer than the other, which has less mass. This has been attributed to the different force with which bodies **attract** the fire; and indeed, nothing is easier than to arrive at such thoughts: since this, however, as it seems to me, cannot be the case, because the **fire particles** possess a **repulsive force** <term>repulsive force (zurückstoßende Kraft): A key concept in early physics where certain "fluids" like fire or electricity were thought to naturally push away from one another, rather than being attracted.</term>, and thus are not capable of any attraction <note>original: "Zug." In this context, it refers to the pull or "suction" of attraction.</note>. It seems that people generally have looked only at the presence of fire within these bodies, and have therefore concluded that those bodies must be warmer which hold more fire in themselves. And then, of course, nothing was more probable than that the denser bodies must attract the fire more strongly, because they become warmer than the less dense ones. ### §. 50.

From what we have said in sections 48 and 49, it is evident that a body composed of very many elastic parts requires far more force for its compression than another that consists of only a few elastic parts. If one assumes, however, that the same force acts on both, then at least the body possessing more elastic parts will resist it longer than the other which has fewer. If it is finally compressed, it must return to its previous position with far greater intensity <note>original: "Gewalt"; in this context, it refers to the magnitude of restorative force</note>, since this is equal to the sum of the force of all its parts. What follows more naturally from this than that a body possessing much mass does not become warm as quickly as another that has less mass? For heat depends either on the quantity of fire particles <term>Feuertheilchen: fire particles; an 18th-century concept where heat was viewed as a fluid or material substance (like "caloric") composed of minute particles</term>,

For elemental fire is distributed evenly throughout the entire universe <note>original: "Weltraum"; here referring to the vast expanse of space according to the physics of the time</note>, and why then should it be more abundant in these specific bodies? Therefore, if elemental fire is not found more abundantly in heated bodies, it must nevertheless move away from them more strongly than from other bodies; and in possessing a greater velocity, it produces a stronger sensation in our nerves. ### §. 52.

We can now easily see how one body can heat or cool another, and what the nature of the apparent movement of fire from one body into another actually is. Let us imagine a body being heated by a flame. The flame consists of liberated combustible parts <note>original: "befreieten brenbaren Theilen"; this refers to the 18th-century chemical view that fire is a physical substance trapped within matter and released during burning</note> which, through their trembling, set the elemental fire <term>Elementarfeuer: a "subtle fluid" or weightless element that scientists of this era believed filled the universe and carried heat</term> in motion, and finally, after the outer crust <note>original: "zerbrochner Rinde"; some early modern scientists imagined atoms or particles as having a shell or "rind" that had to be broken to release the energy or fire within</note> is broken, they release the fire contained within them, which flies apart with the greatest speed. From this, it is clear that the elemental fire moves away from the flame very rapidly. A whole stream of fire, as it were, issues forth from the flame in all directions. Now, imagine another body toward which such a stream of fire rushes; the moving fire-particles will strike partly its solid parts and partly its small interstices <term>Zwischenräumchen: the tiny gaps, pores, or spaces between the solid particles of a substance</term>. Consequently, the fire-particles draw closer together.

When the fire-particles come closer to one another, their repulsive force <note>original: "fortstossende Kraft"; the theory that particles of heat naturally push away from each other, causing expansion</note> is increased, and they move away from each other again with even greater speed. Because of this, they strike the solid parts located within the body and increase their trembling motion <note>this reflects the transition from "caloric" theory (heat as a fluid) toward the kinetic theory of heat (heat as motion)</note>. 

We have stated that while elemental fire, due to its extraordinary subtlety <term>Subtilität: the quality of being so fine and thin that it can pass through solid matter</term>, is capable of penetrating the smallest interstices of bodies, it does not do so on its own because of the repulsive force it possesses. For as a fire-particle strives to move away from the solid parts of a body, it must not enter an interstice if that space is so small that the effect of the fire-particle on the solid parts of the body would become noticeable as it tries to force its way in. If, however, the fire-particle is moved with a force that is greater than its own **repulsive force** <note>original: "fortstossende Kraft"</note>, it will penetrate into this very **interstice** <term>Zwischenräumchen: the microscopic gaps or pores between the solid atoms of a substance</term>. Since the **elemental fire-particles** <term>Elementarfeuertheile: the individual units of the fluid-like substance then believed to constitute fire</term> move very quickly away from the flame, they will then also penetrate into the smallest interstices of bodies that are otherwise empty of fire. Thus, if fire penetrates into even the smallest interstices of a body while it is heated by a flame, there is no doubt that the body should be expanded thereby. 

For the fire-particles act through a process of repulsion: if they penetrate very small interstices of the body, they come very close to its solid parts and must consequently push these so far apart until their **repulsive force** reaches an **equilibrium** with the **cohesive force** <note>original: "zusammenhängende Kraft"; the internal attraction that holds a solid object's particles together</note> of the body's smallest particles. Experience confirms this. For **metals** and other bodies that cannot be dissolved into a flame are expanded by the power of fire; this is why one [has proceeded] not only to the invention of the...

### §. 53.

But what kind of sensation will such a body—which has been near a flame and upon which that flame has acted (as we demonstrated in the previous section)—cause in our **nerves**? We can answer this easily. The **elemental fire** <term>elemental fire: in 18th-century physics, fire was often viewed as a physical substance or fluid that lived within the "pores" of other matter</term> that was already present within the body has been compressed by the newly arriving fire particles; it now expands again. Beyond this, the solid parts of the body have been set into a **vibration** <note>original: "Schwingung"</note> by the impact of the elemental fire particles, and they push away the fire particles located around them from the outside. ### §. 54.

Let us now see how a heated body will act upon another that is still cold. Two cases can occur here. Either the bodies touch each other or they do not. Let us imagine each case. Suppose both bodies touch each other. If two **elastic bodies** A and B touch, and A is compressed, it must expand again by virtue of its **elasticity** <term>Schnellkraft: literally "quick-force," a contemporary term for the restorative force or springiness of a material</term>. Since it touches B, it cannot expand in any other way than by compressing B. However, B is also elastic and will likewise return to its previous shape. It will therefore act back upon A, and this reciprocal action would continue constantly if a portion of the **force** <note>The author uses "Kraft" (force) in a sense that modern readers might associate with energy or momentum.</note> were not lost every time. If a portion of the force is lost every time, then the force must eventually be lost entirely, and both bodies will cease their compressing and expanding. It is easy to see that I am assuming here that A and B are fixed in the place where they lie and cannot move away; for otherwise, these two bodies would immediately move apart from one another. If A [moves] very

For a specific amount of time is required for the communication of force. We see this tangibly in the well-known experiment where a tobacco pipe is hung from a scale and smashed very quickly. Because the tobacco pipe breaks here sooner than the motion could be communicated to the beam of the scale, the scale—even if it is very sensitive—will not be moved. This could not happen if the impact were impressed from one body to the other in an instant—that is, in a truly infinitely small amount of time. 

Therefore, if body A vibrates very rapidly, the impact during its expansion will not be immediately communicated to body B. This will only happen after many impacts, as can be easily understood from what was proven in §. 48. This is driven forth from a heated body, and when a cold body touches a warm one, the <term>elementary fire</term> set in motion by the warm body undoubtedly penetrates into the small interstices of the other. And because the <term>elementary fire</term> already present within it is thereby compressed, it is thus set into motion and helps to increase the heat of the body.

### §. 55.

Where does it come from, one might ask me, that two bodies which have the same degree of heat yet do not seem equally warm to us if they possess different <term>densities</term> <note>Original: *Dichtigkeiten*. In 18th-century physics, density was closely linked to the amount of "matter" in a given space, which influenced how heat was perceived to flow.</note>? Water seems much colder to us than air, although thermometers teach us that they possess the same degree of heat. Must one not admit here a transition of the fire from a less dense body into a denser one, and consequently a "draft" of fire—or at least a coherence of the same? I do not doubt that one can answer this question without assuming a draft of those bodies into the fire. Suppose A and B were two equally warm bodies, but A possessed more <term>mass</term> than B. Our body is indisputably warmer than the air in its natural state; let us therefore suppose that our body is also warmer than A and B. Let us now assume that we touched both bodies and see what changes both will cause in us. Body A possesses more mass than B; it will therefore resist the motion, by virtue of its <term>inertia</term> <note>Original: *Trägheit*. We wish to consider the second case (§. 54.): how two bodies act upon one another when they do not touch. Here, two cases can again be determined. Either there is another body between the two, or there is not. The first case occurs most commonly. ...speed with which light moves, [one concludes] rightly that the particles of the **solar body** <note>The physical matter of the Sun.</note> are in a state of trembling motion, through which they act upon the light and drive it away from themselves with force. We observe that very strongly heated bodies, whose parts are violently shaken, begin to act upon light; we therefore conclude that the movement of light takes its origin from the trembling of bodies. Light recedes in straight lines from the luminous solar body, and this motion can produce heat in two ways.

Firstly, it is highly probable that **elementary fire** <term>Original: *Elementarfeuer*. In 18th-century natural philosophy, this was considered a subtle, fluid-like substance (sometimes called "caloric") that permeated the universe and was the material essence of heat.</term> is spread throughout the entire universe. It must, therefore, also fill the space between the Sun and us; and in such a case, it cannot be otherwise: the light flowing through this very space must act upon the elementary fire and set it in motion. The particles of elementary fire all possess a **repulsive force** <note>Original: *fortstossende Kraft*. A mechanical property where particles push away from one another.</note>. The repulsive force of a single particle of fire is insignificant when compared to the violence with which an entire ray of light moves. If a ray of light thus approaches a particle of fire, the latter will strive to [push] it away from itself...
