If a body is now exposed to rays of light, many rays must necessarily strike its **interstices** <note>Original: *Zwischenräumchen*. The microscopic pores or empty spaces within a solid object.</note>. Now, all bodies are surrounded by elementary fire. The fire particle situated immediately in front of the body's interstice must seek to distance itself from the light, and therefore retreats into the body, as we have already seen above (§ 52). Consequently, more fire particles are driven into the body by the light than would ordinarily find space within it. The elementary fire already located within the body is thus compressed and must seek to expand again; therefore, the body must seem warm to us (§ 53).

A 18th-century term for what we now understand as molecular or atomic vibration caused by energy transfer.</note>, which eventually becomes so strong that the body begins to act upon the fire surrounding it and pushes it away, by which means heat is likewise produced

Why else would the heat in summer be so variable? At one moment we have warm days, and at the next, cold ones, even though the height of the sun remains nearly the same, and one thus cannot derive this change from the sun’s altitude at all. The sultry summer days, in which the heat often becomes unbearable, would hardly occur if certain **vapors** <note>Original: *Dünste*. In 18th-century natural philosophy, this referred to various gaseous exhalations or particles suspended in the atmosphere.</note>, which contain a great deal of **combustible matter** <note>Original: *Brenbares*. This refers to the substance of fire or "phlogiston" believed to be stored within physical particles.</note>, did not fill the air. Why else would the heat be diminished after thunderstorms? And how else would it happen that even in our northern lands the nights are often uncommonly warm, despite the sun being absent and unable to cause this warmth? During the day, the sun's rays can act upon the combustible matter located in the air in such a way that, through their force, its **rind** <note>Original: *Rinde*. The author imagines combustible particles as having a hard outer shell or "rind" that traps the "elementary fire" inside.</note> is broken, and the fire contained within is set free and spreads in all directions. 

However, since a multitude of salty, sulfurous, moist, and other vapors are found in the air, these can easily be set into an internal motion and a kind of **fermentation** <term>fermentation: here used in its archaic sense to mean a chemical agitation or internal boiling of particles rather than a biological process</term> by the heat of the sun's rays; during this process, the rubbing of the parts against one another breaks the rind of the combustible matter, and the heat is increased. motion can also continue, even if the sun is gone; so it is no wonder if the heat also continues at night in summer. Especially since the nights in summer are very short in our regions, and the **small particles** <note>Original: *Körperchen*. These are the "corpuscles" or microscopic building blocks of matter described in early modern physics.</note> located in the atmosphere do not have time to cool down completely.

### §. 60.

Now one will no longer wonder at the powerful effect of **burning glasses** <term>burning glasses: convex lenses used to concentrate sunlight to a point to ignite objects or melt materials</term> and **burning mirrors**, which ignite in an instant and melt the hardest bodies, or transform them into **lime** <note>Original: *Kalk*. In historical chemistry, this refers to "calx," the powdery substance (oxide) left over after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly burned or "calcined."</note> and glass. For here, a double force is united. A vast quantity of light rays strikes the solid parts of such bodies and causes a very strong **vibration** <note>Original: *Schwingung*. The author views heat as a mechanical vibration of the internal parts of an object.</note> within them. At the same time, an even greater number of these rays penetrates into the small interstices <note>Small gaps or spaces between the solid parts of a material.</note> of the body; and since the **elementary fire** <term>elementary fire: the pure, substance-like element of fire believed to exist in the spaces between particles of matter</term> surrounding the body gives way to the motion of the light and is thus likewise pushed into the interstices of the body, the fire located within it must be uncommonly and strongly compressed thereby. On the subject of burning glasses, I wish to share with my readers a particular observation made by **Boerhaave** <note>Herman Boerhaave (1668–1738) was a famous Dutch physician and chemist; his work was foundational for 18th-century science.</note>, who noted that **burning glasses** act far more powerfully in winter than in summer. This seems quite paradoxical. One would think that the sun must act much more strongly in summer because its rays then fall less obliquely <note>Meaning the sun is higher in the sky, so its rays hit the earth more directly rather than at a "slanting" or oblique angle.</note> upon our regions. However, although this should increase the effect of the sun's rays through the burning glasses, this increase is weakened again by other causes. **Naturalists** <term>naturalists: or *Naturkündiger*, a term for scientists who studied physics, chemistry, and biology before these were distinct fields</term> have already provided various reasons for this phenomenon, and one must admit that many factors must be considered together if this occurrence is to be properly explained. In summer, even on clear days, the air is filled with many vapors that originate from the great heat of the sun. These must necessarily catch and reflect back many solar rays. Furthermore, in summer, like all bodies, **mirrors** are expanded by the heat; their interstices <note>The tiny gaps or pores between the solid parts of a material.</note> become wider, and they swallow up many rays that they would otherwise reflect...

### Thoughts on Fire

...would. <note>Original: *würden*, completing the thought from the previous page that many rays would be lost or diverted.</note> However, there is one cause that, it seems to me, one must not overlook. Light moves in straight lines, and the light coming from the sun—due to its uncommonly great distance—moves in parallel lines. If this movement of light is altered; if the rays cannot move parallel to one another; then they also cannot be collected into a single point, and the **burning glasses** and **burning mirrors** must become weaker. In summer, the heat is increased by the **combustible matter** <term>combustible matter: *Brenbare*, referring to the flammable particles or "phlogiston" believed to be suspended in the atmosphere</term> present in the air (§. 59.). The fire contained within this matter is set free and moves in various directions. Who does not see that, because of this, many light rays must be hindered in their motion? For the liberated **elementary fire** <term>elementary fire: *Elementarfeuer*, considered by 18th-century scientists to be the pure, fluid substance of heat itself</term> moves very quickly. It will therefore collide with many rays with force and give them a different direction, so that they cannot be collected into a single point by the **burning glasses** and **burning mirrors**. Thus, the more **combustible matter** there is in the air, and the warmer the air is: the more the effect of the **burning glasses** and **burning mirrors** must be diminished.

->§. 64.<-

I must speak just one more word regarding the origin of **combustible matter**. It is not at all probable that the Creator produced all combustible matter at once during the creation of the world, since it is certain that much combustible matter is constantly consumed, and eventually a lack of it would arise if the loss were not replaced and new combustible parts were not generated. One can easily imagine the possibility of this generation, and here too one must admire the wisdom of the Creator, who has placed the things of the world into such a connection that the destruction of one body must contribute something to the production of another. # Thoughts on Fire

suppose that a tiny gap were just large enough that two **elementary fire particles** <note>original: "Elementarfeuertheile."</note> would fill it completely, if an external force were to drive them in. If now, through the force of a flame or a glowing body, two fire particles were driven side-by-side into such a gap, they would both become lodged within it. If this happens to many fire particles simultaneously, a quantity of **elementary fire** <note>original: "Elementarfeuer."</note> must become enclosed within a shell formed by the solid parts of the body. Nothing more than this is required for the production of **combustible matter** <note>original: "das Brennbare." In 18th-century chemistry, this referred to the substance or principle (often called phlogiston) that allowed a material to burn.</note>. Thus, when a body burns, this very process of combustion causes a new supply of combustible matter to be gathered in other bodies, thereby replacing the loss that was sustained. This occurs all the more readily within the earth because there—due to various **vapors** <note>original: "Dämpfe."</note>—a near-constant warmth and often even flaming fire exists. Furthermore, there are countless solid bodies present in which the scattered fire can accumulate and, in the manner we have explained, be transformed into combustible matter.

### §. 65.

From what we have said so far, both regarding the **combustion** and the **glowing** of bodies, one can easily draw a conclusion as to how it must happen when, on the one hand, a flame is extinguished and, on the other, glowing bodies are rapidly cooled. The flame is extinguished as soon as its nourishment is withdrawn. For since it consists entirely of **combustible parts** <note>original: "brenbaren Theilen." The author views fire as a physical substance composed of particles.</note>, whose shell <note>original: "Rinde." Referring to the theory on the previous page that fire is trapped inside a physical "rind" or shell.</note> is torn in an instant, and whose effect immediately ceases as soon as that shell is torn; the flame must disappear as soon as the combustible parts have burst, unless new combustible parts immediately take the place of the previous ones.

It is with a flame as it is with **flowing water**. This water seems to us to remain constantly the very same body, even though its parts incessantly push and displace one another. The flame appears to us not to change, even though it is renewed at every moment. A **tallow candle** <note>original: "Unschlitlicht." Tallow is rendered animal fat, the standard fuel for candles before the dominance of paraffin.</note> burns as long as the tallow is made liquid and rises up through the **capillary tubes** <note>original: "Haarrörchen," literally "hair-tubes." The author is describing the physical process of capillary action in a wick.</note> of the wick. If this ascent is prevented by a foreign body, then the light must be extinguished. Such a body is

# Thoughts on Fire

<margin>Water.</margin> Water particles are significantly heavier than air particles; they thus resist the rising of the tallow <note>original: "Unschlit." Rendered animal fat used as fuel for candles.</note> and therefore prevent the liberation of the combustible matter. However, it appears that water acts upon the fire in yet another way besides this. Because water particles are very small, they can cling to the parts of the shell <note>original: "Rinde." The author envisions combustible material as having a physical "rind" or casing that traps the fire inside.</note> of the combustible substance and increase their cohesion. If the cohesion of the parts making up the shell of the combustible substance is increased, the elementary fire <note>original: "Elementarfeuer." This refers to the historical concept of fire as a distinct, material element trapped within matter.</note> will not be released, and the flame must disappear. Furthermore, water diminishes fire by reducing the vibration <note>original: "Erschütterung." This is a fascinating early scientific transition from seeing fire as a substance to seeing heat as a kinetic motion of particles.</note> of the solid parts of the burning body. Through this vibration, not only was the elementary fire set in motion, but the combustible matter itself was pushed forth. We have already heard above that heat is lost through contact with another body because the trembling motion <note>original: "zitternde Bewegung."</note> is communicated to the other body and is thus diminished in the first. Therefore, a glowing body will cease to glow if it is thrown into water or if water is poured upon it.

§. 66.

### §. 66.

Why does water, however, increase the flame in certain cases? We notice this when one sprinkles only a little water into the fire, and blacksmiths <note>original: "Schmiede." Skilled metalworkers who use a forge.</note> are accustomed to using this to very great advantage to increase the heat <note>original: "Gluth." This refers to the intense, incandescent glow of a furnace or forge.</note>. This occurrence seems not so easy to explain, because it is not at all probable that the fire is nourished by the water, or that the water particles are so dissolved that they could be considered fire particles <note>This refers to the historical debate over whether water was a fundamental element or could be transformed into fire or "inflammable air" (hydrogen).</note>.

If one may speculate, it seems very probable to me that the matter can be explained in this way.

As long as the flame burns, combustible parts constantly rise upward through the tubes <note>original: "Röhren." Natural philosophers of this era believed that wood and coal possessed microscopic channels or pores through which flammable matter flowed.</note> of the wood or the coals. If the outermost combustible particle is prevented from fully detaching itself from the burning body, then it also cannot serve as nourishment for the flame; but at the same time, all the remaining combustible parts that were about to follow it are held back within the body. If this hindrance is so great that it cannot be overcome by the fire, the flame is extinguished; however, if the hindrance is finally removed, the flame must necessarily become larger.

For the combustible matter <note>original: "das Brenbare." ->§. 67.<-

Just as water, through its weight <note>original: "Schwere." In this context, it refers to both gravity and the physical density of the liquid pressing down.</note>, hinders the rising of the combustible particles, the same must also occur through other liquid bodies, even if they themselves possess combustible parts. Brandy <note>original: "Brandtewein." Literally "burnt wine," referring to distilled spirits.</note> extinguishes a glowing coal if it is poured cold upon it; and even lamps themselves are extinguished by too great a quantity of oil. This is because, in these cases, the brandy and the oil act merely according to their weight, and the flame does not have time to liberate the combustible matter contained within these liquid bodies. If, however, this matter is finally liberated by the force of the fire, it is easy to see that the flame must then be greatly increased. For there come...

...[come] the combustible parts of the liquid body to the combustible parts of the burning body, and these must therefore together occupy a larger space. One can clearly observe this in a burning tallow candle <term>tallow candle: a candle made from rendered animal fat (suet), which was the primary material for candles before paraffin was developed</term>. If one lets only a few drops of melted tallow fall onto the flame, the flame will become very small; indeed, the candle will be in danger of going out entirely. In 18th-century physics, this refers to the physical pressure and gravity of the liquid suppressing the flame's movement.</note>—from its effect in regard to the combustible parts it contains.

### §. 69.

Since fire consists partly in the **vibration** <note>original: "Erschütterung." The author is describing heat as a mechanical motion of particles.</note> of the parts of a body, and partly in the actual motion of the **elementary fire**, it is no wonder that one can also produce fire through **rubbing** <note>friction</note>. For since the surfaces of bodies are not perfectly smooth, the protruding parts must strike against each other during rubbing. This happens with all the greater force the greater the speed is with which the bodies move over one another. Through this striking, the solid parts must necessarily be shaken, and thus become capable of acting not only upon the elementary fire, but also upon the light. Therefore it happens that metals and similar solid and dense bodies can be made completely glowing hot through rubbing. ### §. 70.

This experience—that bodies can be dissolved into a flame through rubbing—leads us to another phenomenon which has occupied the most famous natural scientists for several years. Specifically, it has been found that some bodies, when rubbed, emit a light and, furthermore, attract and repel light bodies, and even ignite easily flammable substances. One will easily see that I am speaking of nothing other than **electricity**, which by **Gilbert** <note>William Gilbert (1544–1603), an English physician and natural philosopher who was among the first to distinguish between magnetism and "the electric force" (static electricity).</note>

->§. 71.<-

Since the electric matter <term>electric matter</term> <note>original: "elektrische Materie." For we have already heard above that combustible matter is of very different kinds, and cannot always be composed of an equal number of elementary fire particles, because it acts upon light with unequal force, thereby causing the differences in colors.

### Thoughts on Fire

...where the electric matter <term>electric matter</term> <note>18th-century scientists believed electricity was a physical fluid or "matter" that filled the pores of solid objects.</note> was previously located, these spaces become empty when it is pushed out; thus, the elementary fire <term>elementary fire</term> <note>In early physics, this was considered a weightless, fluid element responsible for heat and light.</note> must forcefully rush into these spaces from the surrounding air. This process is exactly what appears to cause the attraction of light bodies. For as a fluid matter moves toward a certain place, it carries along any light bodies that stand in its way. The elementary fire must therefore drive very light bodies—such as gold leaf, small pieces of paper, and the like—toward the electrified body, making it appear to us as though the body is pulling them toward itself.

->§ 74.<-

The electric matter surrounds the electrified body, and since it is a type of combustible substance <note>original: "Brennbaren." The author categorizes electricity as a subset of the substances that cause combustion or fire.</note>, and such substances act by pushing outward, the individual parts of the electric matter must also seek to distance themselves from one another. This expansion would actually occur if the air did not resist its movement and compress the electric matter—which surrounds the body like an atmosphere—in the same way we have observed with a flame. From this, one will recognize how the communication <note>original: "Mittheilung." This refers to the transfer or conduction of electricity from one object to another.</note> of electricity occurs.

Bodies have small interstices <note>original: "Zwischenräumchen." These are the microscopic pores or gaps between the physical particles of an object.</note> of varying sizes. If the small interstices of the bodies are smaller than the particles of air; then empty spaces <note>original: "luftleere Räume." Literally "air-empty spaces," referring to a vacuum.</note> must arise within the body itself, because air cannot fill such spaces that are smaller than its own elementary parts. For this surrounds bodies on all sides and, due to its expansive power, must penetrate into their small interstices, where it finds little or no resistance. Since there is no air in the smallest interstices, the elementary fire enters them unhindered. Those bodies in which **electricity** can be excited through rubbing contain **electric matter** <term>electric matter</term> <note>18th-century scientists conceptualized electricity as a physical fluid that resided in the "pores" of matter.</note> in their smallest interstices, which is pushed out by the vibration of the parts; this creates empty spaces within them. ### Thoughts on Fire

the tearing of the crust of the **combustible matter** <note>original: "Brenbaren." In 18th-century chemistry, this refers to the substance within a body that allows it to burn, often associated with the theory of Phlogiston.</note>, however, a movement must arise, which is communicated to the finger and its nerves all the more because the finger directly touches the **electric atmosphere** <note>The field of "electric fluid" believed to surround a charged object.</note>.

If other combustible parts are near the electric atmosphere, then the violent movement arising from the **electric sparks** can tear their crust and produce a **flame**, as is customary to happen when warmed **spirit of wine** <term>spirit of wine</term> <note>An early term for highly concentrated ethanol or alcohol.</note> is touched by an electrified finger. When one warms the spirit of wine, it gives off **vapors** <note>original: "Dünste."</note>, and who would doubt that there is much combustible matter in these vapors? For the combustible is volatile, and since in a liquid body the cohesion of the parts is not very great, the combustible matter will also not cohere and will therefore be more easily liberated. <meta>The text on this page continues a discussion on the behavior of electricity and light within a vacuum, likely following a description of an air pump or electrostatic generator experiment.</meta>

# Thoughts on Fire

[In]side the hollow body is reduced, and the <term>electric matter</term><note>The author uses "elektrische Materie," reflecting the 18th-century "fluid theory" where electricity was viewed as a physical substance that could flow and be compressed like a liquid.</note> must therefore be pushed toward where there is the least resistance. One can easily conclude from this that a light must arise in a <term>vacuum</term><note>original: "Luftleeren Raum" — literally "air-empty space."</note> if the electric matter is enclosed within it. For the parts of the electrical body are in a trembling motion; they thus push the electric matter away from themselves, and this matter would spread out if it had the space to do so. However, the pressure of the air keeps it together in the vacuum and prevents its expansion. Thus, as often as the electric matter tries to move out through the tiny interstices of the body, it is pushed back in again by the pressure of the air. It will therefore continue its movement within the body. This must necessarily result in a collision of the parts of the electric matter, a tearing of their <term>crust</term><note>original: "Rinde" — referring to a contemporary theory that particles of "matter of fire" or electricity had an outer layer that, when ruptured by friction or collision, released light.</note>, and thus the creation of light. For these experiments, a glass sphere is used with great convenience, because light rays pass freely through the glass, and one can therefore better observe the changes occurring within the sphere. If the air is let back into a sphere that is illuminated from within in this manner, then the

<term>female fire</term>. In a living
animal, on account of the constant

and burns just like other bodies that possess much
combustible matter. The <term>phosphorus</term><note>During this era, phosphorus was famously isolated through the distillation of concentrated urine, which served as proof to contemporary scientists that the "principle of fire" resided within the human body.</note>

# Thoughts on Fire

would they have ended up in the urine, if 
one did not have to look for them in the blood itself, 
since the urine, like all other fluids 
of the body, is separated from the blood. 
It is therefore in the blood, and especially 
in its <term>red globules</term><note>original: "rothen Kugeln" — an early term for red blood cells, which had been observed under microscopes since the late 17th century by Leeuwenhoek and others.</note>, that the 
<term>combustible matter</term><note>original: "das Brenbare" — referring to phlogiston, the substance then believed to be the essence of fire and inflammability.</note> is to be found. 
Since the blood, as long as an animal lives, is 
constantly moved, these red blood 
globules must constantly rub against the membranes of the 
vessels. Through this, not only is 
something of them worn away, and 
thus the combustible parts freed; 
but through the rubbing, the 
solid parts of the blood globules are also shaken, and 
push out the combustible matter 
contained within them. Hence it happens that the 
body of a living animal is constantly 
filled with a quantity of the released combustible matter. 
Since, during <term>electrification</term><note>The process of charging a body with static electricity, usually via a friction machine.</note>, 
the <term>electric matter</term> penetrates 
the body being electrified and shakes 
its solid parts—whereby an atmosphere 
of electric matter is formed around the 
body (§. 74)—it follows that a body 
possessing many combustible parts must, during 
its electrification, simultaneously 
push out many combustible parts. 
The "rind" <note>The author envisions combustible matter as being trapped within a physical shell or "rind" that must be broken to release the fire within.</note> of these parts is easily 
torn by the movement of the electric matter, 
and the <term>elemental fire</term> can thereby 
be set into such a motion
